Hari nama sankirtanam jayati!

@@ ii | Sequence (Pantati) |
Summary meaning
This page explains the order (pantati) in which a Divyanama bhajan is sung: Dhyana Slokam and Thodayamangalam first, then Ashtapadis, then the Panchapati songs of the great saints, then the Pooja and Upachara songs, and finally the Divyanama Sankeertanam itself, ending with a third Ashtapadi, Dehi Mangalam and a Prarthana Abhang.
Meaning, verse by verse
1–2. Dhyana Slokam, Thodayamangalam and Ashtapadis
– We first meditate on the Lord and the Gurus, and then sing a few Ashtapadis of Jayadeva as time allows.
3. Panchapati
– Songs of Narayana Theerthar, Bhadrachala Ramadasa, Purandara Dasa, Sadasiva Brahmendra, Gopalakrishna Bharathi, Thyagaraja, Kabir Das and Meerabai, in that order.
4. Pooja and Upachara songs
– The Lord is worshipped as an honoured guest through songs.
5. Traditional Divyanama songs
– From "Raghava Rama Raghava" to "Krishna Rama Govinda", sung in the fixed traditional order.
6. Divyanama compositions
– Kritis of Bhadrachala Ramadasa, Vijaya Gopala, Narayana Theerthar and Thyagaraja, followed by Gopika Geetham, the dance songs (Kolattam, Kummi), a third Ashtapadi, Dehi Mangalam and a Prarthana Abhang.
Spiritual significance
The order is not random: it moves the heart step by step from meditation, through the songs of the saints and worship, into dancing joy and finally into prayer and surrender.

Detailed explanation
Divyanama Sankeerthanam Pantati – why should we learn it?
Divyanama Sankeerthanam is not just a musical tradition.
It is a sacred spiritual journey.
It is a divine path in which the devotion that welled up in the hearts of the great saints
took the form of music.
The Pantati is a carefully arranged, sacred sequence.
It is not a collection of songs put together casually.
It is a spiritual path that takes us into the state of mind of those saints.
From Narayana Theerthar to Thyagaraja Swamigal,
from Meerabai to Purandara Dasa —
the devotion, longing, surrender and joy they experienced —
this Pantati lets us experience all of it once again.

The greatness of the Pantati
Every kirtanam in this Pantati was chosen not merely for its beauty.
It was chosen with a spiritual purpose.
When we sing in this sacred order,
we are not merely singing;
we immerse ourselves completely in a divine experience.
We live again
the waves of devotion that flowed in the hearts of the saints.

Radha Kalyanam – a divine experience
Radha Kalyanam and Divyanama Sankeerthanam
take us to another world.
There the leela of Radha and Krishna
is not just a story;
it becomes an experience that happens within us.
It is personal,
it is intimate,
it is transforming.

The true meaning of learning
Learning this tradition
is not just knowing the songs.
It is awakening the soul.
In every song a saint's longing echoes.
With every step we walk towards the Divine.

Conclusion
In every note we sing,
the tears of a saint's devotion resound.
In every step we take,
we travel on that timeless divine path.

Divyanama Sankeerthanam – Pantati (order of performance)
Now let us look at the Divyanama Sankeerthanam Pantati, the order of the programme that we follow.

1. The beginning
We begin with:
Dhyana Slokam
Thodayamangalam
These two are the sacred beginning that focuses the mind and settles us in the thought of God.

2. Ashtapadi
After this, depending on the time available,
a few Ashtapadis are sung.
These are the living songs of Jayadeva's tradition of devotion.

3. Panchapati
Next we go to the Panchapati section.
This is a very important spiritual sequence.
In it the kirtanams of the saints are sung in this order:
Narayana Theerthar – Tharangam
Bhadrachala Ramadasar kirtanams
Purandara Dasar kirtanams
Sadasiva Brahmendra kirtanams
Gopalakrishna Bharathi
Thyagaraja Swamigal
Kabir Das
Meerabai
All of these are sacred kirtanams that bring out the many rasas of devotion.

4. Pooja and Upachara kirtanams
After the Panchapati,
the Pooja and Upachara kirtanams are sung (this part is yet to be covered in detail).

5. The Divyanama Sankeerthanam section (the main sequence)
Then begins the main Divyanama Sankeerthanam section.
It is traditionally sung in this order:
Raghava Rama Raghava
Sree Rama Sree Rama Surya
Rama Bhadra Rara
Bega Baro
Deva Bandha Namma
Yehi Gopala
Ambe Gowri Mayya
Nama Vaibhava Slokam
Dhanya Dhanya Ho Pradakshina
Paahi Paahi Gajanana
Krishna Rama Govinda

6. Special Divyanama kirtanams
After this we sing
the kirtanams that belong specially to Divyanama.
They are in this order:
Bhadrachala Ramadasar (Bhadradri kirtanams – 5 or 7)
Vijaya Gopalar
Narayana Theerthar
Thyagaraja
Gopika Geetham
Thava Charanambujam
Angana
Gopi Gopala
Joolata Radha
Kolattam
Kummi
Shiva Shiva Bhava
Dharo Jagannatha
Krishna Rama Govinda
A third Ashtapadi
Dehi Mangalam
Any Prarthana Abhang

Closing thought
This Pantati is not just a list of songs.
It is a map of a spiritual journey.
Every stage lifts us higher.
Every Name takes us deeper within.
This is the greatness of Divyanama Sankeerthanam.

@@ iii | Radha kalyana pantati |
Summary meaning
This page explains the meaning behind Radha Kalyanam. Radha stands for bhakti and Madhava for the Supreme; their wedding symbolises the devotee (jivatma) uniting with God (paramatma). The story of Nandagopa, the rain and Radha is told, and the Muthukkuthal ritual is explained as a picture of meditation.
Meaning, verse by verse
Namasankeertanam
– Krishna told Narada that He lives where His devotees sing His Names. In Kali Yuga this is the easiest, purest and surest path, open to everyone.
Radha Kalyanam
– It is Jeeva–Brahma Aikyam: the devotee becoming one with the Lord. Externally it is celebrated like any wedding, for our joy.
The story
– Krishna caused a storm so that Radha would carry Him; then He showed her Goloka, she remembered her true nature, and Brahma performed their wedding with all Vedic rites. So the Kalyanam is done with all the ceremonies.
Muthukkuthal (Kotnotsavam)
– The mortar is the pure mind, the pestle is the sound of Om, and the rice is the soul covered by maya. Pounding the rice with songs of the Lord removes the husk of maya and offers the pure soul to God.
Spiritual significance
If we perform the Kalyanam with this understanding – that it is a meditation in which the soul is purified and offered to God – the Lord's grace is assured.

Detailed explanation
Radha Kalyana Pantati – a spiritual explanation
Radha Kalyanam is not just a festival.
It is a spiritual experience; an expression of the union of the jiva and the Paramatma.
The glory of Nama Sankeerthanam
Bhagavan Sri Krishna firmly told Narada:
"Wherever My devotees do Nama Sankeerthanam, there I always dwell."
In Kali yuga, difficult practices such as yaga and yajna are all hard to do.
But Nama Sankeerthanam is the easiest, purest and surest path.
It needs nothing else.
One thing is enough — a heart full of devotion.
That is why all the scriptures and hymns
guide us to this very path of the Name.

The philosophy of Radha Kalyanam
Radha Kalyanam is the union of the jivatma and the Paramatma.
Here:
Radha — the fullness of devotion (Bhakti Swaroopa)
Madhava (Krishna) — the Paramatma (Brahman)
That is, the state in which devotion reaches God
is the spiritual meaning of Radha Kalyanam.
But outwardly, for our own experience,
it is performed as a wedding festival.
The spiritual meaning of the wedding rituals
The rituals in this festival:
Kotnotsavam (Muthukkuthal)
Varaprekshanam
Choornikai
Pravaram
Kanyadanam
Panigrahanam
Thirumangalya Dharanam
None of these are mere social customs.
All of them are symbols of union with the Paramatma.

The divine leela – the essence of the story
Once Nandagopa carried Krishna on his shoulder and went to the forest to graze the calves.
Then the sky turned dark with clouds; there was lightning and thunder and heavy rain.
Since Krishna was a little boy to Nandagopa,
he thought the child would be frightened and tried to send him home.
At that moment Radha came there.
Nandagopa called Radha, handed the child Krishna to her,
and asked her to take him home safely.
Radha held the child lovingly and walked on.
The divine change
All these changes in nature were brought about by Krishna,
to make Radha realise her true nature (swaroopa).
Suddenly Goloka appeared —
palaces, the divine abode, the whole divine setting was revealed.
Radha and Madhava rejoiced in their true divine forms.
The awakening of the jivatma
Seeing this vision,
Radha realised her true state.
In that bliss she forgot the child
and stretched out both her arms towards Krishna.
At that moment the child vanished.
The one who appeared there was Madhava (Krishna).
The divine arrangement of the wedding
Since Krishna and Radha in human form
had to follow the divine rules,
Bhagavan Krishna called Lord Brahma
and ordered him to perform the wedding at once in the Vedic way.
Lord Brahma conducted that divine wedding:
Pravaram
Kanyadanam
Panigrahanam
Homams
The wedding was completed with every rite laid down in the shastras.
The essence of the Choornikai
This leela is the essence told in the Choornikai.
The truth it shows us is this:
Radha Kalyanam is a complete divine wedding.
It took place in the Vedic way, without any lack.
Closing thought
So there is nothing wrong in performing this Radha Kalyanam
according to the Pantati given by the bhajan gurus.
Because it is:
an expression of devotion
a reminder of the union of the jiva and the Paramatma
the highest state of Nama Sankeerthanam
Conclusion
Radhe Krishna!
Hari Nama Sankeerthanam Jayati!
Kotnotsavam (Muthukkuthal) – a spiritual explanation
Kotnotsavam, or Muthukkuthal, is an important part held very sacred in the Telugu sampradaya.

The outer practice (as seen in the tradition)
In this ritual, rice (raw paddy with its husk) is placed in a wooden mortar called an ural.
It has a hollow at the top and a round base below.
The rice is pounded into powder with a long cylindrical pestle (ulakkai).
The pestle is decorated with flowers, mango leaves and red and white stripes.
The rice powder made this way
is placed in the cloth tied round the waist of the couple.
Then they are given an oil bath (Talandu Sobhanam).
After that, new clothes are given.

The inner spiritual meaning
Within this outer ritual lies a very deep philosophy.
The mortar – our mind
The pestle – the sound of Omkara
The rice – our jivatma
(the pure, sattvic soul in a state covered by maya)
Just as the husk covers the rice,
maya covers the jivatma.

The philosophy of Muthukkuthal
What is the aim of devotion and meditation?
The mind must become pure.
It must be freed from the six faults such as desire and anger.
It must move away from bonds such as the triputi.
For this:
five maidens (the five jnanendriyas – the pure senses)
place the jivatma (the rice) in the mortar.
Five devotees (the five karmendriyas)
pound it with the pestle,
singing the qualities of the Lord with the sound of Omkara.
This is Muthukkuthal.

Removing maya – winnowing the rice
In this process,
the husk (maya) that covers the rice is removed.
What comes out is:
the pure, white jivatma.
That pure soul is then
offered in the presence of the Lord.

The spiritual meaning
This is a very high philosophy:
the jivatma → is freed from maya
the mind → becomes pure
Omkara → resounds within
finally → it attains union with the Paramatma
That is the union of the jiva and the Paramatma.

A picture of meditation
The whole Muthukkuthal process is:
the five senses
the five karmendriyas
the sound of Omkara
the singing of the Lord's glory
all of these together — a process of dhyana yoga.

Closing thought
Though it looks outwardly like a wedding ceremony,
inwardly it is a very high spiritual experience.
If we enjoy the Kalyanam with this attitude,
it becomes like a yagna.
That is the way to receive the Lord's grace.
In this way, Muthukkuthal stands as a sacred symbol
that shows the state of jivan mukti.

Conclusion
Hari Nama Sankeerthanam Jayati!

Musala Nartanam – a spiritual explanation
Musala Nartanam is regarded as one of the highest expressions of devotion in the Divyanama Sankeerthanam tradition.

The outer meaning
Devotees hold a pestle (musala) in their hands
and dance in joy.
It is done together with the raga and tala of the Ashtapadi songs.
Though the scene looks outwardly like a festival,
its inner meaning is very deep.

The inner philosophy
It stands for the blissful state of a jivan mukta.
A jivan mukta is one who,
though in the body,
though living in the world,
keeps the mind always fixed in the Paramatma.
Though engaged in outer actions,
he is immersed within in complete bliss.
That bliss is Musala Nartanam.

The union of the jiva and the Paramatma
In this state:
the jivatma becomes one with the Paramatma
the feeling of duality (dvandva bhava) disappears
Omkara is no longer separate
Brahman, Omkara and the jiva — all are one
So there is no more feeling of separation.
Only the state of Sat-Chit-Ananda, where all has become one, remains.

The state of jivan mukti
One who has reached this state:
will always be in bliss
has peace within, even with actions outside
and life itself becomes a dance
This is the spiritual meaning of Musala Nartanam.

Radha Kalyanam – order of the programme
Ashtapadi Dhyana Slokam + Ashtapadi 22 + Namavali
Rama Rara Sita Rama Rara
Gowri Kalyana Vaibhogame
Sri Krishna Jaya Jaya
Allo Nere Thillo
Suvvi Balakrishna Suvvi
Pahimam Sri Krishna
Suvvi Chandrasekharalu
Chittajuni Kanyaku
Musala Nartanam – Ashtapadi 3
Vasudeva Tanayudu Krishna
Vastram and Aasirvadam
Choornikai
Pravaram + Kanyadanam
Lagnashtakam
Mangalya Dharanam
Anandam Anandam
Namavali – Kolahala
Kalyana Tarangam – "Alokaye Rukmini"
Namavali – Kalyana Krishna
Nalangu kirtanams
Anjaneya kirtanam
Prarthana Abhang

Closing thought
This whole Pantati is not an outer festival.
It is a spiritual journey.
Every song
is a step towards jivan mukti.
In the end we realise only one thing:
All is one
All is bliss
All is Krishna

Conclusion
Hari Nama Sankeerthanam Jayati!

@@ 1.1 | Puṇḍarīkam |
Summary meaning
The Puṇḍarīkam is the call of victory ("jay!") that opens every bhajan. The leader calls out the names of Rama, Shiva and Krishna, then the line of Gurus, all saints and devotees, the gods, Pandarinath and Hanuman, and everyone answers "jay!".
Meaning, verse by verse
jAnakI kAnta smaraNam jay jay rAma rAma
– Remember the Lord of Janaki: victory to Rama!
hara nama: pArvatI patayE hara hara mahAdEva
– Salutations to Hara, the Lord of Parvati: Hara Hara Mahadeva!
gOpikA jIvana smaraNam gOvindA gOvindA
– Remember the very life of the Gopis: Govinda, Govinda!
jagatguru Adi shankara … samasta santa maNDalI kI jay
– Victory to the world-teacher Adi Shankara, to Bodhendra, Sridhara Venkatesa Ayyaval, Marudanallur Sadguru Swamigal, Gnanananda, the Shankaracharyas, Gopalakrishna Bhagavatar, Premikendra Maharaj, and to all the holy men and saints.
vighnEshAdi … AnjanEya svAmI kI jay
– Victory to all the gods beginning with Ganesha, to Pandarinath (Vittala) and to Hanuman.
Spiritual significance
Before singing, we remember the whole family of the Lord, His Gurus and His devotees. Their blessing makes the bhajan theirs, not ours.

@@ 1.2 | Nāmāvaḷi |
Summary meaning
A short chant of the Lord's names that begins the bhajan: "Hari Narayana, remover of sins, supreme bliss, Sadasiva Shankara, beloved of devotees, lotus-eyed one – I am Your servant."
Meaning, verse by verse
hari nArAyaNa
– O Hari, O Narayana.
durita nivAraNa
– Remover of all sins and sorrows.
paramAnanda
– Supreme bliss itself.
sadAshiva shankara
– The ever-auspicious Shankara (Hari and Hara are one).
bhaktajanapriya
– Dear to devotees.
pankaja lOcana
– Lotus-eyed Lord.
tava dAsOham
– I am Your servant.
gOpikA jIvana smaraNam gOvindA gOvindA
– Remember the life-breath of the Gopis: Govinda, Govinda!
Spiritual significance
"tava dāsōham – I am Yours" is the heart of all bhakti; the Namavali places this surrender at the very start.

@@ 2.6.12 | Āj Sakhī Mērē |
Summary meaning
A song of joy by Brahmananda: "Today, my friend, my Sadguru has come to my house, and my heart is full of bliss." The Guru's darshan and words remove sins, poverty, doubts and the fear of rebirth.
Meaning, verse by verse
Aj sakhI mErE satguru ghar AyE / mErE man mE(n) Ananda bhayO rE
– Today, O friend, my true Guru has come to my home; my mind is filled with joy.
darshan sE sab pAp vinAshE / dukh daridra sab dUr gayO rE
– By his darshan all sins are destroyed; all sorrow and poverty have gone far away.
amrita vacana sunat tama nAshyO / ghaTa bhItar prabhu pAya layO rE
– Hearing his nectar-like words, darkness vanished; I found the Lord within my own heart.
janam janam kE samshaya TUTE / bhava bhaya tApa miTAya dayO rE
– The doubts of many births are broken; he has wiped away the fear and burning pain of worldly life.
brahmAnanda dAsa dAsana kO / caraNa kamala lipaTAya rahyO rE
– Brahmananda, servant of servants, clings to his lotus feet.
Spiritual significance
When the Guru comes into our life, God is found not outside but within; the only response is to hold on to the Guru's feet.

@@ 2.6.13 | Varuvāy Varuvāy |
Summary meaning
A loving prayer by Sri Muralidhara Swamigal calling the Guru: "Come, come, O Gurunatha, come every day and bless us. You are mother, father and Guru; we have surrendered and forgotten everything else."
Meaning, verse by verse
varuvAy varuvAy gurunAdA / vandaruL purivAy gurunAdA
– Come, come, O Lord who is my Guru; come and shower Your grace.
anudinam varuvAy gurunAdA / anugraham seyvAy gurunAdA
– Come every day, O Gurunatha, and bless us.
mAtA pitA guru nI tAnE / mana kavalaiyai tIrppadum nI tAnE
– You alone are mother, father and Guru; You alone remove the worries of the mind.
magizhciyai aLippadum nI tAnE / sarva mangaLa mUrtiyum nI tAnE
– You alone give joy; You are the embodiment of all auspiciousness.
kanavu palittadu gurunAdA / manamum kuLirndadu gurunAdA
– My dream has come true, O Gurunatha; my mind has become cool and peaceful.
sharaNam aDaindOm gurunAdA / sakalamum marandOm gurunAdA
– We have taken refuge in You and forgotten everything else.
Spiritual significance
The Guru is everything to the disciple; complete refuge in him brings peace that nothing else can give.

@@ 2.6.14 | Kaṇṇāra Kaṇḍu |
Summary meaning
A Tamil song on Kanchi Maha Periyava (Sri Chandrasekharendra Saraswati): "Seeing him with full eyes, hearing him with full ears, we sing of him with full voice and praise him every day."
Meaning, verse by verse
kaNNAra kaNDu, kAdAra kETTu / vAyAra pADuvOm nam kAnci periyavA
– Seeing him to our eyes' content and hearing him to our ears' content, we sing with full voice of our Kanchi Periyava.
nenjAra uNDu, ninaivAra koNDu / nidam pOTruvOm shrI candrashEkharA
– Enjoying him with all our heart and holding him in our memory, we praise Sri Chandrasekhara every day.
jaya jaya shankara hara hara shankara / kAnci shankara kAmakOTi shankara
– Victory to Shankara of Kanchi, of the Kamakoti Peetham.
shankarar vazhiyil naDaipODum vIrar …
– The hero who walks in the path of Adi Shankara; the fresh, radiant one who removes the pain brought by Kali Yuga.
kAmAkSiyin amsam, kAmakOTi vamsam …
– A part of Goddess Kamakshi, of the Kamakoti line; we worship his twin feet. King of Advaita, treasure of the renunciates – with you as our support there is no sorrow.
Spiritual significance
Seeing, hearing, singing and remembering a great Guru with all our senses is itself a complete sadhana.

@@ 2.6.15 | Kaṇṇāra Kaṇḍu |
Summary meaning
The same tune and words as the previous song, now sung on Sri Ramanujacharya: "Seeing him, hearing him, we sing of Ramanuja with full voice and praise him every day."
Meaning, verse by verse
kaNNAra kaNDu, kAdAra kETTu / vAyAra pADuvOm rAmAnujA
– Seeing and hearing to our heart's content, we sing of Ramanuja.
nenjAra uNDu, ninaivAra koNDu / nidam pOTruvOm nambi rAmAnujA
– Enjoying him in our heart and holding him in memory, we praise Nambi Ramanuja every day.
rAmAnujA engaL rAmAnujA
– Ramanuja, our own Ramanuja!
saDagOpan vazhiyil naDaipODum vIran …
– The hero who walks in the path of Nammazhvar (Sadagopan), our Bhashyakara, the radiant one who removes the pain of Kali Yuga.
ANDALin aNNan, igapara vaNNan …
– Elder brother of Andal, who gives both this world and the next; king of the ascetics, treasure of the renunciates – with you as our support there is no sorrow.
Spiritual significance
The Acharya is the bridge between us and the Lord; praising him with every sense keeps that bridge strong.

@@ 2.6.16 | Vizhi Kiḍaikkumā |
Summary meaning
A heart-felt Tamil prayer to the Guru: "Will I receive your glance? Will I receive your protecting hand and the shade of your feet? When sorrows come wave after wave, will I hear your voice saying 'Do not fear'?"
Meaning, verse by verse
vizhi kiDaikkumA, abhaya karam kiDaikkumA / gurunAdan caraNattil nizhal kiDaikkumA
– Will I get your gracious look, your hand that grants fearlessness, the shade at the feet of my Guru?
alai mIdu alaiyAga tuyar vandu sErum pOdu / "anjAdE" enum kuralai sevi kETkumA
– When sorrows come like wave upon wave, will my ears hear your voice saying "Do not fear"?
kODi janmam nAn eDuppEn guru undan aruL irundAl …
– With your grace I will gladly take a crore of births and make myself fit for you. When unexpected troubles come, will I find that hand which gives protection the moment I think of you?
nangUram pOl gurunAdan kaDai vizhi irukka …
– With the Guru's side-glance as my anchor, why should my mind fear the storm of samsara? I have placed all that is mine at your feet with true love – will I get a place in the boat of your eyes?
Spiritual significance
The Guru's glance is the anchor and the boat across the ocean of samsara; the disciple asks only for that.

@@ 2.6.17 | Kānci Mānagar Pōga Vēṇḍum |
Summary meaning
"I must go to the great city of Kanchi and see our compassionate Lord" – a Tamil song longing to visit the Kamakoti Jagadguru Peetham and its Guru.
Meaning, verse by verse
kAnci mAnagar pOga vENDum / engaL kAruNya mUrtiyai kANa vENDum
– I must go to the great city of Kanchi; I must see our embodiment of compassion.
uttamar vaNangum guru pITam …
– The Guru's seat worshipped by the noble, praised by Siddhas, where the learned gather – the Kamakoti Jagadguru Peetham.
eLimai nirainda guru pITam …
– A seat full of simplicity, worshipped by all, which removes our worries.
maDamaigaL pOkkum guru pITam …
– The seat that removes ignorance, grants beneficial wisdom, where God Himself walks among us (the "walking God", Maha Periyava).
hara hara shankara jaya jaya shankara …
– Victory to Shankara of Kanchi, of Kamakoti.
Spiritual significance
A pilgrimage to the Guru's seat is a pilgrimage to grace; even the longing to go purifies the heart.

@@ 2.6.18 | Tiru Kumaranāy Avatarittār |
Summary meaning
A Tamil song on the birth of Adi Shankaracharya: Shiva left Kailasa and was born as the holy son of Shivaguru and Aryamba, so that the four Vedas could flourish again and the whole world would call him "Jagadguru Shankara".
Meaning, verse by verse
tiru kumaranAy avatarittAr – shankarA / tiru kayilai aganru, shiva guru AryAmbAL
– Shankara was born as the holy son of Shivaguru and Aryamba, leaving sacred Kailasa,
irukku mudal catur maraigaL tazhaikka …
– so that the four Vedas beginning with the Rig might flourish, and all would call him "Jagadguru Shankara, victory!"
nAstika panju malaikku sUrAvaLi yena …
– Like a whirlwind to the cotton-hill of atheism, like the rising sun to the darkness in human hearts, like a rain-cloud for the crop of faith – the beginningless and endless Shiva was born as a Brahmin at Kalady.
shankara guru jaya … shanmata sthApaka
– Victory to Guru Shankara, who established the six systems of worship.
apAra mahima gurunAthA, krpA sAgara gurunAthA …
– Guru of boundless glory, ocean of grace, kind to the humble, full of compassion.
kAlaDiyil avataritta shankara guru / kAla bhayam tIrttu aruLvAy
– Guru Shankara, born at Kalady, remove our fear of time and death.
Spiritual significance
Adi Shankara's coming was Shiva's own grace to revive dharma; remembering his birth fills us with that same light.

@@ 3 | Ashtapadi (note) |
Summary meaning
The book does not print the Ashtapadis here; it gives a link instead. In this app, all the Ashtapadis of Jayadeva's Śrī Gīta Govindam, with lyrics and meanings, are available as a separate book on the home page.
Spiritual significance
The Ashtapadis are sung early in every bhajan; their sweetness prepares the heart for the Divyanama.

@@ 3.1 | Shrī Pārthasārathī Stuti |
Summary meaning
A song by Narayana Theerthar on Krishna as Arjuna's charioteer (Parthasarathi): "Worship Madhava, the treasure of love and grace, dressed as the charioteer of the Pandavas." Krishna bathes and tends Arjuna's horses with His own hands – the Lord serves His devotee.
Meaning, verse by verse
nakha niyamita kaNDUn …
– Opening verse: May Krishna protect us – the treasure of Devaki's merit – who, holding the reins in His teeth, scratches the horses' itching with His nails and bathes them daily with water from His palms.
bhajata prEma krpAnidhi mAdhavam / pANDava sArathI vESam
– Worship Madhava, ocean of love and grace, in the dress of the Pandavas' charioteer.
sAdara mIkSitamamarai rAjau / karatala tOtra vibhUSam
– Watched with reverence by the gods in the battlefield, adorned with the whip in His hand.
kamalAkuca kalashOpari cinham …
– With the same nails that left marks on Lakshmi, He lovingly soothes the victorious horses in battle.
yatkara pallava parikalitAni / shankha gadAbja cakrANi …
– The hands that hold the conch, mace, lotus and discus now pour water to wash the horses' limbs.
yEna sasarja jagadgururAdau / nigama kadambam …
– The mouth that gave out all the Vedas at the beginning now calls "ho! ho!" to the horses.
yatpada pankajam api kamalAyA: …
– The feet that even Lakshmi and the minds of sages find hard to reach walk to Hastinapura as Yudhishthira's messenger.
tyaktvA svadharmam Ashrita dayayA …
– Out of love for His devotee He even set aside His own vow, to keep Bhishma's vow; His curls are full of the dust raised by the horses, and drops of sweat shine on His cheeks.
dIna dayAparamArta sharaNyam …
– Kind to the humble, refuge of the suffering, whose play is to protect devotees, devoted to caring for Kunti's sons – the Gopala of Bhadrachala.
Mangalam: navanIta cOrAya …
– Auspiciousness to the butter-thief, protector of Nanda and the cowherds, beloved of the Gopis, praised by Narada – salutations to Guru Narayana Theerthar.
Spiritual significance
The Lord of the universe gladly becomes a servant for His devotee; this is the highest proof of His love.

@@ 11.12 | Mantra Puṣpam |
Summary meaning
The book only names this item. Mantra Puṣpam is the Vedic hymn ("yō'pāṁ puṣpaṁ vēda…") chanted while offering flowers to the Lord at the end of the pooja. The chant itself is the flower offered.
Spiritual significance
The Veda is offered back to the Lord as a flower – our worship is complete only when it is offered with His own words.

@@ 11.13 | Namaskāram |
Summary meaning
The book only names this item. Here everyone prostrates before the Lord, offering body, mind and speech in surrender.
Spiritual significance
Namaskaram is vandanam, one of the nine forms of bhakti: bowing down melts the ego.

@@ 11.14 | Kaṭṭiya Vacanangaḷ |
Summary meaning
The book only names this item. Kaṭṭiya vacanangaḷ are the formal words of praise announced aloud before the Lord, like the herald's announcements in a king's court, proclaiming His titles and glory.
Spiritual significance
We honour the Lord as the King of kings; announcing His glory reminds us whose court we are standing in.

@@ 11.15 | Caturvēda Pārāyaṇam |
Summary meaning
The book only names this item. Here passages from the four Vedas (Rig, Yajur, Sama and Atharva) are chanted before the Lord as part of the upacharas (services).
Spiritual significance
Hearing the Vedas is itself a purification; they are offered to the Lord from whom they came.

@@ 11.17 | Vinnapa Gadyam |
Summary meaning
The book only names this item. A gadyam is a prayer in prose; the "vinnapa" (humble request) gadyam is a prose prayer in which the devotee humbly places their requests and surrender before the Lord.
Spiritual significance
After singing and serving, the devotee speaks to the Lord simply and directly, as a child to a parent.

@@ 11.38 | Pāmbāṭṭi Siddhar |
Summary meaning
The book only names this item. Pāmbāṭṭi Siddhar was a Tamil Siddha (yogi-poet) whose songs address the "snake" – the kundalini, or the mind – telling it to dance ("āḍu pāmbē") and awaken towards God.
Spiritual significance
The Siddhars teach that the real snake to be charmed is our own restless mind.

@@ 10.7 | Baṇṭu Rīti Kolu Vīyavayya |
Summary meaning
Thyagaraja asks Rama for the post of a royal guard (baṇṭu) in His service. The uniform he wants is horripilation (thrill of devotion), the badge is "I am Rama's devotee", and the sword is Rama's Name.
Meaning, verse by verse
baNTu rIti kolu vIyavayya rAma
– O Rama, please grant me service as Your guard.
tuNTa viNTi vAni modalaina madA / dula goTTi nEla gUla jEyu nija
– (A guard) who strikes down Manmatha and the other proud enemies (lust, pride…) and throws them to the ground.
rOmAnca manu ghana kancukamu / rAma bhaktuDanu mudra biLLayu
– The thrill of devotion as my fine uniform, and the badge that says "Rama's devotee",
rAma nAma manu vara khaDga mivi / rAjillu nayya tyAgarAjuni kE
– and Rama's Name as my excellent sword – these will shine on Thyagaraja.
Spiritual significance
The devotee wants no high position – only to stand guard at the Lord's door, armed with His Name, keeping out the inner enemies.

@@ 10.8 | Vandanamu Raghunandana |
Summary meaning
Thyagaraja bows to Rama: "Salutations, O joy of the Raghus, builder of the bridge, sandal-paste to devotees." He asks why Rama argues with him, says Rama's Name is his daily vow, and begs Him to come quickly.
Meaning, verse by verse
vandanamu raghunandana / sEtu bandhana bhakta candana rAma
– Salutations to You, Raghunandana, who built the bridge, who is cooling sandal to Your devotees.
shrIdamA nAtO vAdamA …
– Is it right to argue with me? Is there any difference between us? Is this a joke, O Rama?
nyAyamA nIkAdAyamA …
– Is this just? Is this how You profit? Will You now neglect me, You who are sung by sages?
kSEmamu divya dhAmamu / nitya nEmamu rAma nAmamu
– Rama's Name is my welfare, my divine abode and my daily observance.
vEgarA karuNA sAgarA / shrI tyAgarAja hrdayAgarA
– Come quickly, O ocean of mercy, who dwells in Thyagaraja's heart.
Spiritual significance
The devotee can argue lovingly with the Lord, but ends in surrender: the Name is enough.

@@ 10.9 | Sītā Kalyāṇa Vaibhōgamē |
Summary meaning
Thyagaraja's famous wedding song: "How splendid is the wedding of Sita, the wedding of Rama!" Each stanza praises Rama's qualities – praised by Hanuman, protector of devotees, hero of all the worlds, born of the solar line.
Meaning, verse by verse
sItA kalyANa vaibhOgamE rAma kalyANa vaibhOgamE
– The grandeur of Sita's wedding! The grandeur of Rama's wedding!
pavanaja stuti pAtra pAvana caritra …
– Praised by Hanuman, of holy deeds, with the sun and moon as eyes, of lovely form; protector of devotees, holding a quiver of arrows, whose play grants both enjoyment and liberation, guardian of the Brahmins.
pAmarAsura bhIma paripUrNa kAma …
– Terror to the wicked demons, whose every wish is fulfilled, dark-hued, delight of the world, Lord of Ayodhya; support of all worlds, the one great hero in battle, far from proud men, firm as the golden mountain.
nigamAgama vihAra nirupama sharIra …
– Who sports in the Vedas and Agamas, of matchless form, who held up the mountain and destroys sins, support of all who bow to Him; whose story is sung even by Shiva, the boat across the ocean of samsara, born in the solar dynasty, praised by Thyagaraja.
Spiritual significance
Singing the divine wedding brings that auspiciousness into our own homes and hearts.

@@ 11.1 | Āyāhi Mādhava |
Summary meaning
Narayana Theerthar invites little Krishna home: "Come, Madhava! O Krishna, child Krishna, darling Krishna!" It is midday, He is tired from play; the devotee offers fresh butter and curd, and describes His lovely feet, eyes and earrings.
Meaning, verse by verse
AyAhi mAdhava / hE krSNA … bAla krSNA, muddu krSNA, cinni krSNA
– Come, Madhava! O Krishna, child Krishna, darling Krishna, little Krishna.
sangava samayE atItE …
– The cow-herding hour is over, the sun is at midday; You are covered in sweat and tired after playing with the cowherd boys.
candra maNDala samAna cArudadhi …
– Please accept in my home curd as white as the moon, foamy pieces and thick fresh butter, O lovely one with tinkling anklets.
pallavAdati kOmaLa pAda pankaja …
– Your lotus feet softer than tender leaves, fragrant like jasmine; Your eyes like full-blown lotus petals, Your fair cheeks – You are the ornament of the Gopis' hearts.
AlOla maNi kuNDala …
– With swaying jewelled earrings and pearl strings, delighting in child-like play, lotus-navelled, fond of butter – Narayana, easily reached by Narayana Theerthar.
Spiritual significance
The Lord of the universe comes as a hungry child; our simplest offering made with love is a feast to Him.

@@ 11.2 | Ēhi Tāta |
Summary meaning
Vijaya Gopala Yati's alphabetic song calling Krishna lovingly as "dear one" (tāta): "Come, dear Vasudeva!" Each of the eighteen lines praises a different quality or deed of Krishna.
Meaning, verse by verse
Ehi tAta shrI vAsudEva Ehi tAta
– Come, dear one, Sri Vasudeva, come!
1–3. Ehi pAhi gOpI jana vallabha …
– Beloved of the Gopis, protect us; lotus-navelled Lord, grant joy; giver of imperishable fruit, punisher of the wicked, who frees us from the fear of samsara; dark as a hill of collyrium, lotus-eyed, who sports in the bowers.
4–8. Isha palAsha vinAshana kEshava …
– Keshava, destroyer of demons, who frees souls from bondage; who protects great renunciates; who crushed Kaliya and saved the elephant; whose soft feet are red with kumkuma, wearing kasturi tilaka; mighty in destroying enemies, cheeks shining with earrings.
9–13. brindAraka muni brinda niSEvita …
– Served by gods and sages, son of Nanda, the butter-thief; who bewitched Shiva and Brahma; with lips like bimba fruit and a conch-like neck, praised by Tumburu; who held Mount Mandara, slew Madhu and Kaitabha, Mukunda with a lotus face; friend of Arjuna, praised by Lakshmana.
14–18. dharavara cakra gadAdharavara …
– Bearer of discus and mace, slayer of Mura; protector of those who surrender, ocean of mercy; terror to demons, giving welfare to His servants; whose lotus feet dwell in the hearts of the good; the sun to the lotus hearts of the Vraja maidens – whose form enchanted Vijaya Gopala.
Spiritual significance
Calling the Lord "tāta" – dear child – is the sweetness of vātsalya bhakti: the infinite God becomes our own little one.

@@ 11.3 | Bārō Murārē |
Summary meaning
Sri Vadiraja Swami calls Krishna: "Come, Murari, child of the Shauri clan! Get up from your meal, your body is covered with dust – enough of play, kind Krishna!" He ends by praising Him as Hayavadana.
Meaning, verse by verse
bArO murArE bAlaka shaurE …
– Come, Murari, child Krishna; I always think of You with joy and courage.
ooTakE ELO maiyellA dhULO …
– Get up for your meal; your whole body is dusty – stop playing now, O compassionate Krishna.
aruNAbja caraNa kaustubhAbharaNa …
– With feet like red lotuses, wearing the Kaustubha gem, supremely generous, resting on the serpent;
sharadindu vadana sarasija nayana …
– with a face like the autumn moon and lotus eyes, dwelling with the Yadus – O Sri Hayavadana.
Spiritual significance
For Vadiraja, Krishna is both the playful child to be fed and the Supreme Hayagriva; love sees no contradiction.

@@ 11.4 | Sharaṇāgata Vatsala |
Summary meaning
Annamacharya: "O loving refuge of those who surrender, easy for all to reach – Purushottama, accept worship from my hands." He wonders how he can bathe, house and feed the Lord who holds all the worlds, and asks Him to accept his small offerings out of love.
Meaning, verse by verse
sharaNAgata vatsala sarva sulabha / puruSOttama nA cEti pUja gaikonavayyA
– O loving one to those who surrender, easily reached by all – Purushottama, please accept worship from my hands.
mummarampu brahmANDamulu mOcE nIku …
– You carry all the three worlds – can I bathe You with water from my little pot? Let Lakshmi's rose-water be Your bath and accept it gladly.
paTTarAni vishvarUpamulu jUpE ninnu …
– You who show the cosmic form – can I keep You in a box or bag? You lived even in Kausalya's womb; take my heart as Your Vaikuntha.
alacarAramula nAragincE nIku …
– Can I offer food from my small pot to You? O Venkatesha with Alamelumanga, accept it as a feast like the one given by Bharadvaja.
Spiritual significance
Our worship can never match the Lord's greatness; He accepts it only because He loves those who surrender.

@@ 11.5 | Jaya Jaya Dēvādi |
Summary meaning
A song of the upacharas (services) to the Lord: sandal paste, garlands, incense, lamp, arati, food, fruits, sweets and betel are offered one by one to Gopalakrishna, "the ocean of grace".
Meaning, verse by verse
jaya jaya dEvAdi dEva vibhO / gOpAlakrSNa krupAjaladhE
– Victory to the God of gods, the all-pervading Gopalakrishna, ocean of grace.
gandham – kastUrI ghanasAra …
– May sandal paste with musk, camphor and fragrant aloe anoint Your feet, O father of Brahma.
puSpamAlA – mandArAmbuja …
– Accept garlands of mandara, lotus, malati and jasmine with a lovely tulasi garland, O dweller of Brindavan.
dhUpam – kanjaja bhavanuta …
– Praised by Brahma and Indra, Madhava, Govinda, Madhusudana, moon-faced Lord – accept this incense; remover of sins, lotus-eyed, Lord of Ayodhya, friend in distress, protector of the helpless.
dIpam – maNigaNa bhUSaNa …
– Adorned with gems, Lord of Mathura, dear to devotees, who frees from samsara, Keshava of pure bliss – accept this lamp.
hArati – bangAru jUpula …
– A beautiful golden arati to Hari, the Lord of Lakshmi, the auspicious Madhusudana, Sri Ranga – mangalam!
mahA nivEdanam, phalam, bhakSyam, tAmbUlam
– Accept rice with honey, ghee and dal, vegetables, five kinds of sweets, payasam and curd; coconut, jackfruit, mango, bananas and oranges; honey, sweets, milk and butter; and betel leaves with areca nut and camphor.
Spiritual significance
Every service done for an honoured guest is done for the Lord with love; the outer offerings stand for the offering of our whole self.

@@ 11.6 | Shōbhānē |
Summary meaning
A short auspicious song: "Shobhanam! All auspiciousness to the holy Lord full of glory."
Meaning, verse by verse
shObhAnE shObhanamE
– Auspiciousness, auspiciousness!
vaibhavamula pAvana mUrtiki
– To the holy form of the Lord, full of splendour.
Spiritual significance
Ending with auspicious words wishes good for the Lord – and so brings good to all.

@@ 11.7 | Jaya Jaya Ārati |
Summary meaning
The camphor arati song: "Victory, victory to Your arati, O Rama, Lord of my life, Raghunatha, Murari." Kabir sings of the Lord of Seshachala with Lakshmi, with Hanuman standing before Him.
Meaning, verse by verse
jaya jaya Arati rAma tumArE / prANanAtha raghunAtha murArE
– Victory to Your arati, O Rama – Lord of my life, Lord of the Raghus, slayer of Mura.
shESAcalapati laccumi nArI / puruSOttama gOvinda murArI
– Lord of Seshachala with Lakshmi, Purushottama, Govinda, Murari.
sanmukha sharaNA hanumanta vIrA / hariguNa gAvE dAsa kabIrA
– Brave Hanuman stands before You in surrender; Kabir, Your servant, sings Hari's glories.
Spiritual significance
Camphor burns away completely and leaves nothing behind – a picture of the ego dissolving in the light of God.

@@ 11.8 | Citta Juni |
Summary meaning
A mangala (auspicious ending) song: "Victory and auspiciousness to Krishna, the joy of the heart, who is the highest goal of devotion" – to Govinda the son of Nanda, to Pattabhirama, and to Kasturi Ranga.
Meaning, verse by verse
citta juni kannayaku jaya mangaLam …
– Victory and auspiciousness to Kannayya (Krishna), the delight of the mind, the highest goal of those devoted to Him.
andamaina candruni nindincu mukhAravinda …
– To Govinda, son of Nanda, the joy of Lakshmi, whose lotus face puts even the lovely moon to shame; to our Lord Pattabhirama, who as child Rama grants welfare and is the abode of all virtues.
kOri bhaktimIri cEri …
– To Kasturi Ranga, Lord of the Shauri clan, full of glory, who comes to those who seek Him with deep devotion.
Spiritual significance
Mangalam is sung to close the bhajan, wishing all good to the Lord – and in doing so, receiving all good.

@@ 11.9 | Anganāmaṇikini |
Summary meaning
A closing mangalam: auspiciousness to Alamelumanga and Venkataramana, to Rama, Gopalakrishna, Hanuman and the Sadguru; then calls to "Mukunda Radhe", "Govinda Radhe", "Kalyani Radhe – protect me", and to Vithoba and Rakhumai.
Meaning, verse by verse
anganAmaNikini ambuja pANiki / mangaLavatikini mangaLam
– Auspiciousness to the jewel among women, who holds a lotus in her hand, the auspicious one.
ranguga venkaTa ramaNuni alamElu / mangaku yeppuDu mangaLam
– Always auspiciousness to Alamelumanga, beloved of the radiant Venkataramana.
mArAma bhadruniki … sadguru svAmiki shubha mangaLam
– Victory and auspiciousness to our Ramabhadra, Ramachandra, Gopalakrishna, the beloved of Radha, to Hanuman and to the Sadguru.
mukunda rAdhE … kalyANi rAdhE mAm pAhi
– O Radha of Mukunda, of Govinda, O auspicious Radha – protect me.
tEja viThObhA rakhumAyI …
– Radiant Vithoba and Rakhumai, the great king Vithoba and Rakhumai.
Spiritual significance
The bhajan ends by blessing the Lord and His consort together; Radha's grace is sought because she leads us to Krishna.

@@ 11.10 | Sundaratē |
Summary meaning
A prarthana abhang by Sant Tukaram describing Vitthala at Pandharpur: "Beautiful is that form, standing on the brick with hands on the hips." Tukaram says this form is all his happiness.
Meaning, verse by verse
sundara tE dhyAna ubhA viTEvarI / karakaTAvarI ThEvUniyAm
– Beautiful is that form to meditate on – standing on the brick, hands placed on the hips.
makarakuNDala taLapati shravaNim / kaNThim kaustubha maNIm virAjIta
– Fish-shaped earrings glitter at His ears; the Kaustubha gem shines at His throat.
gaLAm tuLasI hAra kAmsE pItAmbara / AvaDE nirantara hEmci dhyAna
– A tulasi garland around His neck, yellow silk at His waist – I always love to meditate on this form.
tukA mhaNE mAjhE hEmci sarvasukha / pAhIna shrImukha AvaDina
– Tuka says: this is all my happiness; I will keep looking at that beloved holy face.
Spiritual significance
Tukaram needs no other goal than the sight of Vitthala; such loving meditation is complete bliss.

@@ 11.11 | Dāsāci Sampatti |
Summary meaning
A prarthana abhang of Samarth Ramdas: "The servant's only wealth is Rama, the Lord of Sita; Rama alone is my companion, mother, father, brother, learning, riches, master and knowledge."
Meaning, verse by verse
dAsAcI sampatti, rAma sItApati, jIvAcA sangAti, rAma Eka
– The servant's wealth is Rama, Sita's Lord; the soul's only companion is Rama.
rAma Eka mAtA, rAma Eka pitA, rAma Eka bhrAtA, sahOdara
– Rama alone is mother, Rama alone is father, Rama alone is brother.
sahOdara vidyA, vaibhava kAncana, sarvahA svajana, rAma Eka
– Brother, learning, glory, gold, all my relatives – Rama alone.
rAma Eka svAmI, rAmahA kaivArI, lAbhayA samsArI, rAma Eka
– Rama alone is master, Rama alone is my protector; the only gain in worldly life is Rama.
rAma Eka gnAna, rAma Eka dhyAna, rAma samAdhAna, rAmadAsI
– Rama alone is knowledge, Rama alone is meditation, Rama is peace – says Ramdas.
Spiritual significance
When Rama becomes everything, nothing in the world can be lost; this is complete surrender.

@@ 11.16 | Upacāramulanu |
Summary meaning
Thyagaraja offers royal services to Rama: "Accept these services, O Lord resting on the serpent king" – a jewelled golden umbrella, a pair of moon-white chamaras (fans) and a fragrant flower fan.
Meaning, verse by verse
upacAramulanu cEkonavayya uragarAja shayana
– Please accept these services, O Lord who rests on Adisesha.
capalA kOTi nibhAmbara dhara …
– Wearing silk that shines like a crore of lightning flashes, Lord of Janaki, have mercy on me.
chatram – kapaTa nATaka sUtra dhArivai …
– You direct the whole drama of the world and grant every wish; a golden umbrella studded with countless gems suits only You.
cAmaram – merugu tIgala …
– A pair of chamaras with golden handles, shining like lightning and white as the autumn moon, suits only You.
vyajanam – jAjulu sampangulu …
– A fan fragrant with jasmine, champaka, marjoram and kuruvera suits only You, O Lord praised by Thyagaraja.
Spiritual significance
Royal honours are given to the true King; offering them in song trains the heart to see Rama as its only Lord.

@@ 11.46 | Svajanahrudamala- Mangalam - Kṛṣṇa Gānam |
Summary meaning
A mangalam to the Guru as Krishna: his soft feet blossom like lotuses in the clear lake of his devotees' hearts; he walks majestically, his eyes are wide with compassion, his face cool as the moon. Victory to him who removes the pride of Kali and gives Hari's Name.
Meaning, verse by verse
svajana hrudamala sarOvara vikasita – sarasIruha mrudu caraNa!
– Whose soft feet are lotuses blooming in the pure lake of his devotees' hearts!
gambhIra gamana! karuNAyata nayana – kalAdhara shItala vadana!
– Of majestic gait, eyes wide with compassion, face cool like the moon!
lalita vishAla lalATa vibhAsita – gOpI candana tilaka!
– On his broad, graceful forehead shines the gopi-chandana tilaka!
jaya jaya kalimada haraNa! jaya hari nAma vitaraNa! jaya hitakara mrudu vacana!
– Victory to him who destroys the pride of Kali, who distributes Hari's Name, whose gentle words do only good!
hEmashaila samadhIra udAra! karuNA pArAvAra!
– Steady like the golden mountain, generous, an ocean of compassion!
jaya shrI mAdhurI ramaNa!
– Victory to the beloved of Sri Madhuri!
Spiritual significance
The Guru who gives the Divine Name is honoured as the Lord Himself.

@@ 12.7 | Hārati |
Summary meaning
The same mangala song as 11.8: "Victory and auspiciousness to Krishna, the joy of the heart" – to Govinda, to Pattabhirama and to Kasturi Ranga – sung here with the arati.
Meaning, verse by verse
citta juni kannayaku jaya mangaLam …
– Victory and auspiciousness to Kannayya (Krishna), the delight of the mind, the goal of those devoted to Him.
andamaina candruni nindincu mukhAravinda …
– To Govinda, son of Nanda, whose lotus face outshines the moon; to Pattabhirama, abode of all virtues, who grants welfare.
kOri bhaktimIri cEri …
– To Kasturi Ranga, Lord of the Shauri clan, who comes to those who seek Him with deep devotion.
Spiritual significance
The arati light is waved before the Lord so that we may see Him clearly – and so that His light may fill us.
